class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n=len(edges)
        sc=[0]*n
        mx=0
        ans=0
        for i in range(n):
            sc[edges[i]]+=i
            if sc[edges[i]]>mx:
                mx=sc[edges[i]]
                ans=edges[i]
            elif sc[edges[i]]=mx:   
                if ans>edges[i]:
                    ans=edges[i]
        return ans

Reason in a repair-and-recheck way.

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

Output only the fixed Python code.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(edges)
        sc = [0] * n
        mx = 0

class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(edges)
        score = [0] * n
        for i, e in enumerate(edges):
            score[e] += i
        return score.index(max(score))
```

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(edges)
        score = [0] * n
        max_score = 0
        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            score[edges[i]] += i
            if score[edges[i]] > max_score:
                max_score = score[edges[i]]
                ans = edges[i]
            elif score[edges[i]] == max_score:
                if ans > edges[i]:
                    ans = edges[i]
        return ans
```
This is clean. Should we rename `sc` to `score`, `mx` to `max_score`? Fine. But if "output only fixed code" maybe preserve original variable names to minimize diff. Either is okay. Maybe keep original variable names to avoid introducing changes. But readability not issue. I'd keep original names maybe:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(edges)
        sc = [0] * n
        mx = 0
        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            sc[edges[i]] += i
            if sc[edges[i]] > mx:
                mx = sc[edges[i]]
                ans = edges[i]
            elif sc[edges[i]] == mx:
                if ans > edges[i]:
                    ans = edges[i]

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(edges)
        sc = [0] * n
        mx = 0
        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            sc[edges[i]] += i
            if sc[edges[i]] > mx:
                mx = sc[edges[i]]
                ans = edges[i]
            elif sc[edges[i]] == mx:
                if ans > edges[i]:
                    ans = edges[i]
        return ans
```
This is minimal. Need no trailing explanation.

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(edges)
        sc = [0] * n
        mx = 0
        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            sc[edges[i]] += i
            if sc[edges[i]] > mx:
                mx = sc[edges[i]]
                ans = edges[i]
            elif sc[edges[i]] == mx:
                if ans > edges[i]:
                    ans = edges[i]
        return ans
```

Let's maybe prove invariant more formally:
Before iteration i, for all nodes, score <= mx, and ans is smallest node with score == mx. Initially true. At iteration, only node v=edges[i] changes. Let s = sc[v]+i.
- If s < mx: scores <= mx still, ans unchanged.
- If s == mx: v now has score mx. If v < ans, update ans to v; else ans remains. Thus ans smallest node with score mx.
- If s > mx: new mx = s. Since no previous node had score s (old mx < s), v is the only node with score s. Set mx=s, ans=v.
Thus invariant. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
>
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(edges)
        sc = [0] * n
        mx = 0
        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            sc[edges[i]] += i
            if sc[edges[i]] > mx:
                mx = sc[edges[i]]
                ans = edges[i]
            elif sc[edges[i]] == mx:
                if ans > edges[i]:
                    ans = edges[i]
        return ans
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def edgeScore(self, edges: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(edges)
        sc = [0] * n
        mx = 0
        ans = 0
        for i in range(n):
            sc[edges[i]] += i
            if sc[edges[i]] > mx:
                mx = sc[edges[i]]
                ans = edges[i]
            elif sc[edges[i]] == mx:
                if ans > edges[i]:
                    ans = edges[i]
        return ans
